### WC.07 Materials: Mica, Mica Alt, Acrylic, Smoke


#### Materials used in Windows apps - Windows apps

T1 [Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/apps/design/signature-experiences/materials) · updated: 2024-11-05 · 15 rules, 0 values

| ID | Kind | Rule / claim |
|---|---|---|
| WC.07.R001 | definition | Materials are visual effects applied to UX surfaces that resemble real life artifacts. |
| WC.07.R002 | definition | Windows uses two primary types of materials: occluding and transparent. |
| WC.07.R003 | behavior | Occluding materials, like acrylic and mica, are used as base layers beneath interactive UI controls. |
| WC.07.R004 | behavior | Transparent materials such as smoke are used to highlight immersive surfaces. |
| WC.07.R005 | definition | Acrylic is a semi-transparent material that replicates the effect of frosted glass. |
| WC.07.R006 | behavior | In Windows 11, acrylic has been updated to be brighter and more translucent, allowing for a stronger contextual relationship with the visuals behind it. |
| WC.07.R007 | do | Acrylic is used only for transient, light-dismiss surfaces such as flyouts and context menus. |
| WC.07.R008 | behavior | Acrylic is mode aware; it supports both light and dark mode. |
| WC.07.R009 | definition | Mica is a new opaque material introduced in Windows 11. |
| WC.07.R010 | behavior | Mica surfaces are subtly tinted with the user's desktop background color. |
| WC.07.R011 | behavior | Mica is mode aware; it supports both light and dark modes. |
| WC.07.R012 | behavior | Mica also indicates window focus with active and inactive states as a built in feature. |
| WC.07.R013 | behavior | Smoke emphasizes an important UI surface by dimming the surfaces beneath so that they recede into the background. |
| WC.07.R014 | behavior | Smoke is used to signal blocking interaction below a modal UI such as a dialog. |
| WC.07.R015 | behavior | Smoke is not mode aware; it is always translucent black in both light and dark mode. |

#### Acrylic material - Windows apps

T1 [Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/apps/design/style/acrylic) · updated: 10/02/2026 · 35 rules, 1 values

| ID | Kind | Rule / claim |
|---|---|---|
| WC.07.R016 | definition | Acrylic is a type of Brush that creates a translucent texture. |
| WC.07.R017 | behavior | You can apply acrylic to app surfaces to add depth and help establish a visual hierarchy. |
| WC.07.R018 | definition | Background acrylic reveals the desktop wallpaper and other windows that are behind the currently active app, adding depth between application windows while celebrating the user's personalization preferences. |
| WC.07.R019 | definition | In-app acrylic adds a sense of depth within the app frame, providing both focus and hierarchy. |
| WC.07.R020 | dont | Avoid layering multiple acrylic surfaces: multiple layers of background acrylic can create distracting optical illusions. |
| WC.07.R021 | do | Use background acrylic for transient UI elements. |
| WC.07.R022 | do | For apps with context menus, flyouts, non-modal popups, or light-dismiss panes, we recommend that you use background acrylic, especially if these surfaces draw outside the frame of the main app window. |
| WC.07.R023 | note | Using acrylic in transient scenarios helps maintain a visual relationship with the content that triggered the transient UI. |
| WC.07.R024 | behavior | Many XAML controls draw acrylic by default. MenuFlyout, AutoSuggestBox, ComboBox, and similar controls with light-dismiss popups all use acrylic while open. |
| WC.07.R025 | do | Use in-app acrylic for supporting UI, such as on surfaces that may overlap content when scrolled or interacted with. |
| WC.07.R026 | do | If you are using in-app acrylic on navigation surfaces, consider extending content beneath the acrylic pane to improve the flow in your app. |
| WC.07.R027 | behavior | Using NavigationView will do this for you automatically. |
| WC.07.R028 | dont | To avoid creating a striping effect, try not to place multiple pieces of acrylic edge-to-edge - this can create an unwanted seam between the two blurred surfaces. |
| WC.07.R029 | note | Acrylic is a tool to bring visual harmony to your designs, but when used incorrectly can result in visual noise. |
| WC.07.R030 | do | For vertical panes or surfaces that help section off content of your app, we recommend you use an opaque background instead of acrylic. |
| WC.07.R031 | do | If your vertical panes open on top of content, like in NavigationView's Compact or Minimal modes, we suggest you use in-app acrylic to help maintain the page's context when the user has this pane open. |
| WC.07.R032 | note | Rendering acrylic surfaces is GPU-intensive, which can increase device power consumption and shorten battery life. |
| WC.07.R033 | behavior | Acrylic effects are automatically disabled when a device enters Battery Saver mode. |
| WC.07.R034 | behavior | Users can disable acrylic effects for all apps by turning off Transparency effects in Settings > Personalization > Colors. |
| WC.07.R035 | behavior | In High Contrast mode, users continue to see the familiar background color of their choosing in place of acrylic. |
| WC.07.R036 | behavior | Both background acrylic and in-app acrylic appear as a solid color when the user turns off Transparency effects in Settings > Personalization > Colors. |
| WC.07.R037 | behavior | Both background acrylic and in-app acrylic appear as a solid color when Battery Saver mode is activated. |
| WC.07.R038 | behavior | Both background acrylic and in-app acrylic appear as a solid color when the app runs on low-end hardware. |
| WC.07.R039 | behavior | Only background acrylic will replace its translucency and texture with a solid color when an app window on desktop deactivates. |
| WC.07.R040 | behavior | Only background acrylic will replace its translucency and texture with a solid color when the app is running on Xbox, HoloLens, or in tablet mode. |
| WC.07.R041 | do | It's important to ensure that any text your app presents to users meets contrast ratios (see Accessible text requirements). |
| WC.07.R042 | note | We've optimized the acrylic resources such that text meets contrast ratios on top of acrylic. |
| WC.07.R043 | dont | We don't recommend placing accent-colored text on your acrylic surfaces because these combinations are likely to not pass minimum contrast ratio requirements at the default 14px font size. |
| WC.07.R044 | dont | Try to avoid placing hyperlinks over acrylic elements. |
| WC.07.R045 | note | If you choose to customize the acrylic tint color or opacity level, keep the impact on legibility in mind. |
| WC.07.R046 | do | Do use acrylic on transient surfaces. |
| WC.07.R047 | do | Do extend acrylic to at least one edge of your app to provide a seamless experience by subtly blending with the app's surroundings. |
| WC.07.R048 | dont | Don't put desktop acrylic on large background surfaces of your app. |
| WC.07.R049 | dont | Don't place multiple acrylic panes next to each other because this results in an undesirable visible seam. |
| WC.07.R050 | dont | Don't place accent-colored text over acrylic surfaces. |

| ID | Name | Value | Context |
|---|---|---|---|
| WC.07.V001 | Font size | 14px | Default font size referenced in the guidance against placing accent-colored text on acrylic surfaces. |

APIs: `AcrylicBrush class`, `Background property`, `Window.SystemBackdrop property`, `DesktopAcrylicBackdrop class`

#### Materials overview - Windows apps

T1 [Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/apps/develop/ui/materials) · updated: 08/19/2026 · 15 rules, 6 values

| ID | Kind | Rule / claim |
|---|---|---|
| WC.07.R051 | definition | Materials are visual effects applied to UI surfaces to create depth, hierarchy, and visual interest. |
| WC.07.R052 | note | WinUI 3 provides four mechanisms for applying materials, each suited to a different scenario. |
| WC.07.R053 | definition | `AcrylicBrush` is in-app acrylic only — it blurs XAML content within the app window. |
| WC.07.R054 | dont | It does not show the desktop or other windows behind the app. |
| WC.07.R055 | do | For desktop-see-through effects, use `Window.SystemBackdrop` or `SystemBackdropElement`. |
| WC.07.R056 | behavior | When no material is applied, the window background is a solid color drawn from the active light or dark theme — no blur or translucency. |
| WC.07.R057 | behavior | WinUI 3 falls back gracefully to a solid color when a material cannot be rendered. |
| WC.07.R058 | behavior | Your app doesn't need to detect these conditions — the APIs handle fallback automatically. |
| WC.07.R059 | do | Make sure any `FallbackColor` or theme background you configure reads well as a plain solid color. |
| WC.07.R060 | definition | Mica incorporates the user's desktop wallpaper color into a muted, personalized background. |
| WC.07.R061 | do | It is designed for the main app window background, especially in the title bar and navigation pane areas. |
| WC.07.R062 | behavior | Mica is not affected. |
| WC.07.R063 | definition | Desktop Acrylic shows a live, blurred view of the desktop and content behind the app window, creating a frosted-glass appearance. |
| WC.07.R064 | do | Use it for navigation panes, sidebars, or content panels where you want a translucent in-app effect. |
| WC.07.R065 | dont | The UWP `BackgroundSource = HostBackdrop` property is not available in WinUI 3. |

| ID | Name | Value | Context |
|---|---|---|---|
| WC.07.V002 | DirectX requirement | DirectX 11 | Mica and Desktop Acrylic require DirectX 11 and adequate GPU memory. |
| WC.07.V003 | Windows version | Windows 11 | Mica is available on Windows 11 only and falls back to a solid theme color on Windows 10. |

#### Mica material - Windows apps \| Microsoft Learn

T1 [Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/apps/design/style/mica) · updated: 07/22/2026 · 22 rules, 5 values

| ID | Kind | Rule / claim |
|---|---|---|
| WC.07.R066 | definition | Mica is an opaque, dynamic material that incorporates theme and desktop wallpaper to paint the background of long-lived windows such as apps and settings. |
| WC.07.R067 | definition | Mica Alt is a variant of Mica, with stronger tinting of the user's desktop background color. |
| WC.07.R068 | do | We recommend that you apply Mica or Mica Alt as the base layer of your app, and prioritize visibility in the title bar area. |
| WC.07.R069 | behavior | The Mica materials are designed for performance as they capture the background wallpaper only once to create their visualizations. |
| WC.07.R070 | behavior | In High Contrast mode, users continue to see the familiar background color of their choosing in place of Mica or Mica Alt. |
| WC.07.R071 | behavior | The Mica materials will appear as a solid fallback color (`SolidBackgroundFillColorBase` for Mica, `SolidBackgroundFillColorBaseAlt` for Mica Alt) when the user turns off transparency in Settings > Personalization > Color. |
| WC.07.R072 | behavior | The Mica materials will appear as a solid fallback color when Battery Saver mode is activated. |
| WC.07.R073 | behavior | The Mica materials will appear as a solid fallback color when the app runs on low-end hardware. |
| WC.07.R074 | behavior | The Mica materials will appear as a solid fallback color when an app window on desktop deactivates. |
| WC.07.R075 | behavior | The Mica materials will appear as a solid fallback color when the Windows version is below 22000. |
| WC.07.R076 | do | We encourage you to apply Mica as the base layer of your app and add an additional content layer that sits on top of the base layer. |
| WC.07.R077 | do | The content layer should pick up the material behind it, Mica, using the `LayerFillColorDefaultBrush`, a low-opacity solid color, as its background. |
| WC.07.R078 | do | The `LayerFillColorDefaultBrush` should be applied to the container backgrounds of your WinUI 3 app surfaces (e.g. Grids, StackPanels, Frames, etc.). |
| WC.07.R079 | do | To give your app's window a seamless look, Mica should be visible in the title bar if you choose to apply the material to your app. |
| WC.07.R080 | do | We encourage you to apply Mica Alt as the base layer of your app when you need contrast between title bar elements and the commanding areas of your app (e.g. navigation, menus). |
| WC.07.R081 | do | The commanding layer should pick up the material behind it, Mica Alt, using the `LayerOnMicaBaseAltFillColorDefaultBrush`, a low-opacity solid color, as its background. |
| WC.07.R082 | do | The content layer should pick up the layers below it, using the `LayerFillColorDefaultBrush`, another low-opacity solid color. |
| WC.07.R083 | do | To give your app's window a seamless look, Mica Alt should be visible in the title bar if you choose to apply the material to your app. |
| WC.07.R084 | do | Set the background to transparent for all layers where you want to see Mica so the Mica shows through. |
| WC.07.R085 | dont | Don't apply backdrop material more than once in an application. |
| WC.07.R086 | dont | Don't apply backdrop material to a UI element. The backdrop material will not appear on the element itself. |
| WC.07.R087 | behavior | The backdrop material will only appear if all layers between the UI element and the window are set to transparent. |

| WC.07.R088 | definition | Mica is an opaque material that incorporates the user's theme and desktop wallpaper to create a highly personalized appearance. |

| WC.07.R090 | behavior | The material helps users focus on the current task by falling back to a neutral color when the app is inactive. |
| WC.07.R091 | claim | Mica can be applied as the base layer of a Win32 app, prioritizing application and visibility in the title bar area. |
| WC.07.R092 | do | To use Mica in a Win32 app, use the MicaController class. |
| WC.07.R093 | claim | The MicaController manages rendering of the system backdrop material and handling of system policy for the Mica material. |
| WC.07.R094 | behavior | The MicaController reacts to the system Light and Dark themes by default. |
| WC.07.R095 | do | FallbackColor, LuminosityOpacity, TintColor, and TintOpacity can be passed to MicaController to override its default theme behavior. |
| WC.07.R096 | do | To enable Mica, you need a reference to the Windows App SDK, a Compositor, and a DispatcherQueue. |
| WC.07.R097 | claim | Mica requires a compositor, which also requires a dispatcher queue. |
| WC.07.R098 | claim | The Mica controller needs a target with a root to recognize the visual base layer. |
| WC.07.R099 | do | Set a root before enabling Mica. |
| WC.07.R100 | do | Assert that Mica is supported by the window and target. |
| WC.07.R101 | do | For WebView2, initialize WinRT as single-threaded because WebView2 is inherently single-threaded. |
| WC.07.R102 | do | For unpackaged apps, reference the Windows App SDK, install WinRT, and have a matching Windows App Runtime Redistributable installed. |

#### System backdrops (Mica/Acrylic) - Windows apps

T1 [Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/apps/develop/ui/system-backdrops) · updated: — · 27 rules, 8 values

| ID | Kind | Rule / claim |
|---|---|---|
| WC.07.R103 | definition | Mica is an opaque material that incorporates the user's theme and desktop wallpaper to create a highly personalized appearance. |
| WC.07.R104 | claim | Mica only captures the background wallpaper once to create its visualization, and is designed for performance. |
| WC.07.R105 | do | We recommend Mica for the foundation layer of your app, especially in the title bar area. |
| WC.07.R106 | definition | Acrylic is a semi-transparent material that replicates the effect of frosted glass. |
| WC.07.R107 | do | Acrylic is used only for transient, light-dismiss surfaces such as flyouts and context menus. |
| WC.07.R108 | do | To apply Mica or Acrylic, set the SystemBackdrop property to a XAML SystemBackdrop, typically MicaBackdrop or DesktopAcrylicBackdrop. |
| WC.07.R109 | do | Mica is typically used as the backdrop for an app Window. |
| WC.07.R110 | do | To use the Mica Alt variant, set the Kind property to BaseAlt. |
| WC.07.R111 | do | Desktop Acrylic can be used as the backdrop for a Window. |
| WC.07.R112 | do | Acrylic is also commonly used as the backdrop for transient UI, like a flyout. |
| WC.07.R113 | claim | SystemBackdropElement requires Windows App SDK 2.0 or later. |
| WC.07.R114 | behavior | Window.SystemBackdrop applies a backdrop material across the entire window; use SystemBackdropElement to apply Mica or Acrylic to a specific region of the UI. |
| WC.07.R115 | definition | SystemBackdropElement renders a system backdrop material within its own bounds. |
| WC.07.R116 | do | You can place SystemBackdropElement anywhere in the XAML tree, set its size explicitly or let it fill its parent, and use CornerRadius to apply rounded corners. |
| WC.07.R117 | do | Place SystemBackdropElement as a sibling behind other content, or as a background host inside a Grid. |
| WC.07.R118 | behavior | You can change the SystemBackdrop property at runtime. |
| WC.07.R119 | behavior | SystemBackdropElement applies a system backdrop to an in-page element, not the window; the two approaches are independent and can be used together in the same app. |
| WC.07.R120 | do | Starting with Windows App SDK 1.3, setting Window.SystemBackdrop to a XAML SystemBackdrop is the recommended way to apply a material for most apps. |
| WC.07.R121 | claim | Composition MicaController and DesktopAcrylicController APIs give you more control over backdrop behavior. |
| WC.07.R122 | behavior | MicaController and DesktopAcrylicController manage both rendering of the system backdrop material and handling of system policy for the material. |
| WC.07.R123 | behavior | The controller reacts to the system Light and Dark themes by default. |
| WC.07.R124 | behavior | After customizing any of the controller's four properties, it no longer applies default Light or Dark values when SystemBackdropConfiguration.Theme changes; you need to manually update those properties to match the new theme. |
| WC.07.R125 | do | Check MicaController.IsSupported or DesktopAcrylicController.IsSupported at runtime to ensure the system supports the backdrop material. |
| WC.07.R126 | do | Provide a target that implements ICompositionSupportsSystemBackdrop; in a XAML app, the XAML Window implements this interface and is used as the backdrop target. |
| WC.07.R127 | definition | A SystemBackdropConfiguration object provides app-specific policy information to configure the system backdrop material. |
| WC.07.R128 | do | An available Windows.System.DispatcherQueue is required on the main XAML thread; call DispatcherQueue.EnsureSystemDispatcherQueue() to ensure one exists. |
| WC.07.R129 | do | Dispose the Mica/Acrylic controller when the window closes so it does not try to use the closed window. |

| WC.07.R136 | definition | These options are used by the DwmGetWindowAttribute and DwmSetWindowAttribute functions. |
| WC.07.R137 | behavior | DWMWA_NCRENDERING_ENABLED retrieves whether non-client rendering is enabled; the retrieved value is BOOL. |
| WC.07.R138 | behavior | DWMWA_NCRENDERING_POLICY sets the non-client rendering policy using a value from DWMNCRENDERINGPOLICY. |
| WC.07.R139 | behavior | DWMWA_TRANSITIONS_FORCEDISABLED enables or forcibly disables DWM transitions; TRUE disables transitions and FALSE enables them. |
| WC.07.R140 | behavior | DWMWA_ALLOW_NCPAINT controls whether content rendered in the non-client area is visible on the frame drawn by DWM. |
| WC.07.R141 | behavior | DWMWA_CAPTION_BUTTON_BOUNDS retrieves the caption-button bounds in window-relative space. If the window is minimized or otherwise not visible, the retrieved RECT is undefined; check whether it contains a usable boundary. |
| WC.07.R142 | behavior | DWMWA_NONCLIENT_RTL_LAYOUT specifies whether non-client content is right-to-left mirrored. |
| WC.07.R143 | behavior | DWMWA_FORCE_ICONIC_REPRESENTATION forces an iconic thumbnail or peek representation, even if a live or snapshot representation is available. It is normally set during window creation and not changed during the window's lifetime. |
| WC.07.R144 | behavior | DWMWA_EXTENDED_FRAME_BOUNDS retrieves the extended frame bounds rectangle in screen space. |
| WC.07.R145 | behavior | DWMWA_HAS_ICONIC_BITMAP informs DWM that the window will provide a bitmap for an iconic thumbnail or peek representation. It can be specified with DWMWA_FORCE_ICONIC_REPRESENTATION and is normally set during window creation. |
| WC.07.R146 | behavior | DWMWA_DISALLOW_PEEK prevents peek functionality for the window; FALSE allows it. |
| WC.07.R147 | behavior | DWMWA_EXCLUDED_FROM_PEEK prevents a window from fading to a glass sheet when peek is invoked; FALSE gives normal behavior. |
| WC.07.R148 | behavior | DWMWA_CLOAK makes a window not visible to the user while it is still composed by DWM. |
| WC.07.R149 | behavior | DWMWA_CLOAKED retrieves a value explaining why a window is cloaked: by its owner application, by the Shell, or through inheritance from its owner window. |
| WC.07.R150 | behavior | DWMWA_FREEZE_REPRESENTATION freezes the thumbnail image with its current visuals; DWM does not further update it to match the window contents. |
| WC.07.R151 | behavior | DWMWA_USE_HOSTBACKDROPBRUSH enables or disables host backdrop brushes for a non-UWP window using Windows::UI::Composition APIs. |
| WC.07.R152 | behavior | DWMWA_USE_IMMERSIVE_DARK_MODE allows the window frame to use dark mode colors when the system dark mode setting is enabled. Windows default to light mode for compatibility; TRUE honors dark mode and FALSE always uses light mode. |
| WC.07.R153 | behavior | DWMWA_WINDOW_CORNER_PREFERENCE specifies the rounded-corner preference for a window using DWM_WINDOW_CORNER_PREFERENCE. |
| WC.07.R154 | behavior | DWMWA_BORDER_COLOR specifies the window-border color. The app is responsible for changing it when state changes, such as window activation. DWMWA_COLOR_NONE suppresses the border; DWMWA_COLOR_DEFAULT restores the system default. This makes a rounded window with no border possible. |
| WC.07.R155 | behavior | DWMWA_CAPTION_COLOR specifies the caption color; DWMWA_COLOR_DEFAULT restores the system default behavior. |
| WC.07.R156 | behavior | DWMWA_TEXT_COLOR specifies the caption-text color; DWMWA_COLOR_DEFAULT restores the system default behavior. |
| WC.07.R157 | behavior | DWMWA_VISIBLE_FRAME_BORDER_THICKNESS retrieves the width of the outer border DWM would draw around the window; the value can vary with the window DPI. |
| WC.07.R158 | behavior | DWMWA_SYSTEMBACKDROP_TYPE retrieves or specifies the system-drawn backdrop material of a window, including behind the non-client area. |
| WC.07.R159 | behavior | DWMWA_REDIRECTIONBITMAP_ALPHA enables or disables use of the alpha channel in the window's redirection bitmap. When enabled, pixels must contain premultiplied alpha; when disabled, alpha is ignored and the bitmap is treated as fully opaque. It defaults to false. |
| WC.07.R160 | behavior | DWMWA_BORDER_MARGINS specifies the override location of the window border as distances inward from each edge. All-zero values disable the override; otherwise, every value must be at least 1. Non-zero margins force a border, set its location, and clip window contents covered by or outside it. |
| WC.07.R161 | definition | DWMWA_LAST is the maximum recognized DWMWINDOWATTRIBUTE value, used for validation. |
| WC.07.R162 | claim | The minimum supported client is Windows Vista (desktop apps only); the minimum supported server is Windows Server 2008 (desktop apps only). |

| ID | Kind | Rule / claim |
|---|---|---|
| WC.07.R163 | definition | Mica is not a transparency effect; Microsoft calls it a “Backdrop Material” or “Surface.” |
| WC.07.R164 | behavior | Mica is designed to show a blurred version of the user's wallpaper as the background layer. |
| WC.07.R165 | claim | Mica is part of Windows 11's design; performance is described as a benefit, not the reason for it. |
| WC.07.R166 | claim | Using Mica on only one window allows for a better visual hierarchy at a glance. |
| WC.07.R167 | behavior | Windows changes other elements when a window is focused or unfocused, including icon opacity, shadow opacity, and the title bar. |
| WC.07.R168 | claim | Windows 10 also limited Acrylic to one window at a time. |
| WC.07.R169 | definition | Mica appears on the backdrop of an application, behind its other content. |
| WC.07.R170 | definition | Mica is an opaque material that incorporates the user's theme and desktop wallpaper. |
| WC.07.R171 | behavior | As the user moves a window, Mica dynamically adapts using the wallpaper underneath the application. |
| WC.07.R172 | behavior | Mica falls back to a neutral color when the app is inactive. |
| WC.07.R173 | definition | Acrylic is a semi-transparent material that replicates frosted glass. |
| WC.07.R174 | claim | In Windows 11, Acrylic has been updated to be brighter and more translucent. |
| WC.07.R175 | do | Acrylic is used only for transient, light-dismiss surfaces such as flyouts and context menus, according to the quoted guidelines. |
| WC.07.R176 | claim | Acrylic is also used in areas outside flyouts, including the Start Menu, Widgets, Search, and other taskbar popups. |
| WC.07.R177 | behavior | Mica blurs the user's wallpaper; Acrylic can then blur that backdrop and window controls when a user opens a menu that uses Acrylic. |
| WC.07.R178 | claim | The post says developers have limited say in the overall design, which is attributed to UI and UX designers and design guidelines. |
| WC.07.R179 | claim | The post says replacing Mica with Acrylic is not as simple as “Replace Mica with Acrylic.” |
| WC.07.R180 | claim | Acrylic may have a problem working with WINUI, and the post's author believes non-UWP apps cannot use it; the author says Mica can be used by these apps. |
| WC.07.R181 | claim | The post says the Taskbar currently uses Acrylic. |
| WC.07.R182 | claim | The post says the choice between Mica and Acrylic is explicitly covered by UI guidelines, not arbitrary. |
| WC.07.R183 | claim | A comment says the original leaked build had an unintentional Acrylic taskbar. |
| WC.07.R184 | claim | A comment says users can turn Mica off. |

### WC.08 Iconography (Segoe Fluent Icons)


#### Iconography in Windows - Windows apps \| Microsoft Learn

T1 [Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/apps/design/iconography/) · updated: 03/07/2026 · 18 rules, 7 values

| ID | Kind | Rule / claim |
|---|---|---|
| WC.08.R001 | definition | Application icons represent your app in the Windows shell. |
| WC.08.R002 | do | App icons should represent your app's core functionality through a metaphor. |
| WC.08.R003 | do | Use system icons inside your app UI for items like command bars, navigation, or status indicators. |
| WC.08.R004 | definition | All glyphs in Segoe Fluent Icons are drawn in a monoline style. That means they're created through a single stroke of 1 epx. |
| WC.08.R005 | note | Glyphs in Segoe Fluent Icons follow three aesthetic principles: |
| WC.08.R006 | do | Minimal: Glyphs contain only the details that are necessary to communicate the concept. |
| WC.08.R007 | do | Harmonious: Glyphs are based on simple and geometric forms. |
| WC.08.R008 | do | Evolved: Glyphs use modern metaphors that are easily understood. |
| WC.08.R009 | definition | Each font glyph is designed so that the footprint of the icon area is a square em. |
| WC.08.R010 | behavior | An icon with a 16-epx font size is the equivalent of a 16x16-epx icon, to make sizing and positioning more predictable. |
| WC.08.R011 | definition | Base icons are the main element of a visual metaphor. |
| WC.08.R012 | do | Base elements should occupy the entire icon footprint. |
| WC.08.R013 | definition | Modifier icons modify the meaning of the base icon. |
| WC.08.R014 | do | Modifier elements should be placed in one of the bottom quadrants of the icon footprint. |
| WC.08.R015 | do | We recommend using icon layering to create a different state of the same icon (for example, an active or selected state). |
| WC.08.R016 | do | Understand the cultural connotations of symbols. |
| WC.08.R017 | note | Although iconography doesn't require localization in most cases, certain icons might be acceptable in one culture but not in another. |
| WC.08.R018 | do | Validate your iconography choices with the context in which you'll use them. |

| WC.08.V003 | Stroke width | 1 epx | Segoe Fluent Icons glyphs are created through a single stroke. |
| WC.08.V004 | Font size | 16 epx | Example font size used to explain icon sizing. |

#### PUA E700-E9F9

T1 [Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/apps/design/iconography/segoe-fluent-icons-font) · updated: 10/14/2026 · 18 rules, 4 values

| ID | Kind | Rule / claim |
|---|---|---|
| WC.08.R019 | behavior | With the release of Windows 11, the `Segoe Fluent Icons` font replaced `Segoe MDL2 Assets` as the recommended symbol icon font. |
| WC.08.R020 | do | `Segoe MDL2 Assets` is still available, but we recommend updating your app to use the `Segoe Fluent Icons` font. |
| WC.08.R021 | definition | Most of the icons included in the `Segoe Fluent Icons` font are mapped to the Private Use Area of Unicode (PUA). |
| WC.08.R022 | definition | The PUA range is a non-standardized range of Unicode that allows font developers to define their own characters. |
| WC.08.R023 | dont | Icons in the `Segoe Fluent Icons` font are not intended for use in-line with text. |
| WC.08.R024 | behavior | All glyphs in `Segoe Fluent Icons` have the same fixed width with a consistent height and left origin point, so layering and colorization effects can be achieved by drawing glyphs directly on top of each other. |
| WC.08.R025 | note | Many of the icons also have mirrored forms available for use in languages that use right-to-left text directionality such as Arabic, Dari, Persian, and Hebrew. |
| WC.08.R026 | do | If you are developing an app in XAML, you can use specified glyphs from `Segoe Fluent Icons` with a SymbolIcon and the Symbol enumeration. |
| WC.08.R027 | do | If you would like to use a glyph from the `Segoe Fluent Icons` font that is not included in the Symbol enum, set it as the Glyph property of a FontIcon control. |
| WC.08.R028 | do | For optimal appearance, use these specific sizes: 16, 20, 24, 32, 40, 48, and 64. |
| WC.08.R029 | dont | Deviating from these font sizes could lead to less crisp or blurry outcomes. |
| WC.08.R030 | note | On Windows 11: There's nothing you need to do, the font comes with Windows. |
| WC.08.R031 | note | On Windows 10: `Segoe Fluent Icons` is not included by default on Windows 10. You can download it from the Design resources page. |
| WC.08.R032 | dont | You can download the font for use in design and development, but you may not ship it to another platform. |
| WC.08.R033 | note | The standalone version of `Segoe Fluent Icons` available from the Design resources page can lag behind the version that ships with Windows 11, so it might not include every glyph listed in this article. |
| WC.08.R034 | do | If a glyph shown here appears as a missing-character box when you use a downloaded copy of the font, use the version of `Segoe Fluent Icons` that comes with an up-to-date Windows 11 installation, which contains the current set of glyphs. |
| WC.08.R035 | behavior | Glyphs with prefixes ranging from E0- to E5- (e.g. E001, E5B1) are currently marked as legacy and are therefore deprecated. |
| WC.08.R036 | note | The Unicode code points and names of refreshed icons are unchanged, so no code changes are required. |

#### Iconography - Fluent 2 Design System

T1 [Fluent 2 (Microsoft)](https://fluent2.microsoft.design/iconography) · updated: — · 28 rules, 8 values

| ID | Kind | Rule / claim |
|---|---|---|
| WC.08.R043 | definition | Icons represent concepts, objects, or actions, and have semantic purpose within a layout. They should always be recognizable, functional, and easily understood. |
| WC.08.R044 | claim | Fluent iconography is familiar, friendly, and modern to ensure an inviting, intuitive experience. |
| WC.08.R045 | definition | The icon collections are system, product launch, and file icons. |
| WC.08.R046 | do | Use system icons inside a UI for items like command bars, navigation, or status indicators. |
| WC.08.R047 | claim | System icons are open-source under the MIT License. |
| WC.08.R048 | behavior | Regular themed icons are used primarily as a wayfinding device to help people identify and select available actions. |
| WC.08.R049 | behavior | Filled theme icons are used for highlighting selected states or for smaller moments that need a little more weight and readability. |
| WC.08.R050 | claim | Icon size will vary based on use case and platform. |
| WC.08.R051 | dont | Smaller icons can give information or reinforce an idea, but should not be used for interaction. |
| WC.08.R052 | do | When choosing size, consider the way someone might move through an experience. The more precise the tool at hand, the smaller the icon can be. |
| WC.08.R053 | do | Use 12 pixel icons to give information; they’re generally too small for interactions. |
| WC.08.R054 | do | Use larger icons on smaller screens where people may need a larger touch target. |
| WC.08.R055 | definition | Product launch icons represent Microsoft apps. Their primary role is to identify a Microsoft capability and the collaboration and productivity it enables. |
| WC.08.R056 | behavior | Product launch icons can also allow people to take an action, like buying, downloading, or launching the app. |
| WC.08.R057 | dont | Product launch icons draw a connection to the Microsoft brand, but they do not represent the company and should never be used in place of the Microsoft logo. |
| WC.08.R058 | behavior | As product icons scale below 48 pixels, they simplify in detail in favor of readability. |
| WC.08.R059 | do | Use product icons at specific sizes, instead of scaling them, to create pixel-perfect results. |
| WC.08.R060 | do | When scaling product icons larger than 48 pixels, use the full-fidelity icon and scale by factors of four. |
| WC.08.R061 | claim | Multicolor file type icons look best at 16, 48, and 96 pixel sizes in most Microsoft UIs. |
| WC.08.R062 | claim | File type icons are available as SVGs and .webps. SVGs are vectors and can be resized easily, but not all browsers support SVGs. PNGs have wider browser support but require many sizes to remain visually crisp. |
| WC.08.R063 | definition | Fluent system icons are literal metaphors and are named for the shape or object they represent, not the functionality they provide. |
| WC.08.R064 | behavior | When necessary, modifiers can be added to icons to express a more specific metaphor. |
| WC.08.R065 | do | Modifiers are always the filled theme for best contrast and should always be placed in the bottom right corner of the icon. |
| WC.08.R066 | dont | Don’t use modifiers if they will introduce too much complexity in the visual representation or the meaning of the icon. |
| WC.08.R067 | do | If including color on an icon, only use one color and keep contrast in mind. |
| WC.08.R068 | do | Only use solid colors on system icons. |
| WC.08.R069 | dont | Never change the color of product launch icons. |
| WC.08.R070 | do | Understand the cultural connotations of certain symbols in different cultures and validate iconography choices with the context in which they will be used. |

| ID | Name | Value | Context |
|---|---|---|---|

| WC.08.V017 | 2 | two | System icons support two themes: Regular and Filled. |

| WC.08.V019 | Product icon size threshold | 48 pixels | Product icons simplify in detail below 48 pixels; above 48 pixels, use the full-fidelity icon. |
| WC.08.V020 | Product icon scale sizes | 48, 64, 96 pixels | When scaling product icons larger than 48 pixels, scale by factors of four; the page lists 48, 64, and 96. |

#### Icons in Windows apps - Microsoft Learn

T1 [Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/apps/design/style/icons) · updated: 2024-07-29 · 28 rules, 15 values

| ID | Kind | Rule / claim |
|---|---|---|
| WC.08.R071 | do | Use an icon for actions, like cut, copy, paste, and save, or for items on a navigation menu. |
| WC.08.R072 | do | Use an icon if it's easy for the user to understand what the icon means and it's simple enough to be clear at small sizes. |
| WC.08.R073 | dont | Don't use an icon if its meaning isn't clear, or if making it clear requires a complex shape. |
| WC.08.R074 | do | To add an icon in your XAML app, use either an IconElement or an IconSource. |
| WC.08.R075 | definition | IconElement is a FrameworkElement, so it can be added to the XAML object tree, but it can't be added to a ResourceDictionary and reused as a shared resource. |
| WC.08.R076 | definition | IconSource is not a FrameworkElement, so it can't be used as a standalone element in the UI, but it can be shared as a resource. |
| WC.08.R077 | definition | IconSourceElement wraps an IconSource so you can use it anywhere you need an IconElement. |
| WC.08.R078 | behavior | Icon properties with Source in the name take an IconSource; other icon properties take an IconElement. |
| WC.08.R079 | behavior | Rather than specifying a FontFamily directly, FontIcon and SymbolIcon use the font family defined by the SymbolThemeFontFamily XAML theme resource. |
| WC.08.R080 | behavior | By default, SymbolThemeFontFamily uses the Segoe Fluent Icon font family; on Windows 10, version 20H2 or earlier, it falls back to Segoe MDL2 Assets. |
| WC.08.R081 | do | If a glyph is available as a Symbol, you can use a SymbolIcon anywhere you would use a FontIcon with the default font family. |
| WC.08.R082 | behavior | You can only use Symbol names to set an icon property using shortened attribute syntax. |
| WC.08.R083 | behavior | If you don't specify a FontFamily, or specify one that isn't available at runtime, FontIcon falls back to the default font family defined by SymbolThemeFontFamily. |
| WC.08.R084 | claim | Windows 11 introduces Segoe Fluent Icons, a system icon font with more than 1,000 icons designed for the Fluent Design language. |
| WC.08.R085 | claim | Windows font display technology means font icons look crisp and sharp on any display, at any resolution, and at any size. |
| WC.08.R086 | claim | Animated icons can draw attention to entry points, provide feedback from state to state, and add delight to an interaction. |
| WC.08.R087 | behavior | AnimatedIcon can implement lightweight, vector-based icons with motion using Lottie animations. |
| WC.08.R088 | claim | PathIcon can create custom vector-based icons that always look sharp. |
| WC.08.R089 | behavior | Bitmap images have to be scaled depending on the desired icon size and screen resolution; shrinking can make them blurry, and enlarging can make them blocky and pixelated. |
| WC.08.R090 | behavior | BitmapIcon strips out image color information by default and renders non-transparent pixels in the Foreground color. |
| WC.08.R091 | do | For a monochrome BitmapIcon, use a solid image on a transparent background in PNG format. |

| WC.08.R098 | do | To use animated icons, see AnimatedIcon. |

| WC.09.R008 | definition | Timing, easing, directionality, and gravity work together to form the foundation of Fluent motion. |
| WC.09.R009 | do | Each has to be considered in the context of the others, and applied appropriately in the context of your app. |
| WC.09.R010 | definition | Automatic tween and timing between values in a parameter change achieve very simple Fluent motion using the standardized values. |
| WC.09.R011 | behavior | System components, such as common controls and shared motion, are "Fluent by default". Fundamentals have been applied in a manner consistent with their implied usage. |
| WC.09.R012 | do | There may be times when the system does not yet provide an exact motion solution for your scenario. In those cases, use the baseline fundamental recommendations as a starting point for your experiences. |
| WC.09.R013 | definition | Implicit animations are a simple way to achieve Fluent motion by automatically interpolating between the old and new values during a parameter change. |
| WC.09.R014 | behavior | You can implicitly animate changes to the following properties: UIElement.Opacity, UIElement.Rotation, UIElement.Scale, UIElement.Translation, and Background on Border, ContentPresenter, or Panel. |
| WC.09.R015 | do | Each property that can have changes implicitly animated has a corresponding transition property. To animate the property, you assign a transition type to the corresponding transition property. |
| WC.09.R016 | note | Implicit animations require Windows 10, version 1809 (SDK 17763) or later. |
| WC.09.R017 | note | The information and examples in this article are optimized for apps that use the Windows App SDK and WinUI 3, but are generally applicable to UWP apps that use WinUI 2. |

#### Motion in Windows - Windows apps \| Microsoft Learn

T1 [Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/apps/design/signature-experiences/motion) · updated: 02/28/2026 · 21 rules, 21 values

| ID | Kind | Rule / claim |
|---|---|---|
| WC.09.R018 | definition | Motion in Windows is reactive, direct, and context appropriate. It provides feedback to user input and reinforces spatial paradigms that support way-finding. |
| WC.09.R019 | do | Elements that change position and size should visually connect from one state to another, even if they aren't connected under the hood. |
| WC.09.R020 | do | Surfaces that share the same UI entry point should invoke and dismiss the same way to bring consistency to interactions. |
| WC.09.R021 | do | Each transition should respect the timing, easing, and direction of other elements so a surface feels cohesive. |
| WC.09.R022 | behavior | Clear indicators show the system recognizes and adapts gracefully to different input, postures, and orientations. |
| WC.09.R023 | do | Apps should build on OS behaviors to feel responsive, alive, and aid usage depending on input methods. |
| WC.09.R024 | definition | Motion adds personality and energy to the experience in order to transform simple actions into moments of delight. |
| WC.09.R025 | behavior | These moments are always brief and fleeting, and help reinforce user actions. |
| WC.09.R026 | dont | Avoid custom animations where possible. |
| WC.09.R027 | do | Use animation resources like WinUI 3 controls for page transitions, in-page focus, and micro interactions. |
| WC.09.R028 | do | If you can't use WinUI controls, mimic existing OS behaviors based on where the app entry point lives. |
| WC.09.R029 | do | Page transitions, connected animations, and animated icons are the recommended WinUI controls that add delightful and necessary motion to apps. |
| WC.09.R030 | definition | Windows motion is fast, direct, and context-appropriate. |
| WC.09.R031 | behavior | Timing and easing curves are adjusted based on the purpose of the animation to create a coherent experience. |
| WC.09.R032 | do | Add these controls to your app to help better organize information, and help your app's users transition from page to page, layer to layer, and state to state of an interaction. |
| WC.09.R033 | do | Use page transitions to transition smoothly from page to page, and configure animation directions to respect the flow of an app. |
| WC.09.R034 | definition | Page transitions guide your user's eyes to incoming and outgoing content, lowering cognitive load. |
| WC.09.R035 | do | Use connected animations to highlight specific pieces of information within a page or surface, while retaining context. |
| WC.09.R036 | definition | Connected animations give focus to selected elements, and seamlessly transition between the focused and non-focused states. |
| WC.09.R037 | do | Use animated icons to implement lightweight, vector-based icons and illustrations with motion using Lottie animations. |
| WC.09.R038 | definition | Animated icons draw attention to specific entry points, provide feedback from state to state, and add delight to an interaction. |

### WC.10 Control states (rest/hover/pressed/disabled/focus)


#### Guidelines for visual feedback - Windows apps

T1 [Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/apps/develop/input/guidelines-for-visualfeedback) · updated: 09/27/2026 · 21 rules, 8 values

| ID | Kind | Rule / claim |
|---|---|---|
| WC.10.R001 | do | Use visual feedback to show users when their interactions are detected, interpreted, and handled. |
| WC.10.R002 | do | Try to limit modifications of a control template to those directly related to your design intent, as extensive changes can impact the performance and accessibility of both the control and your application. |
| WC.10.R003 | dont | Don't use touch visualizations in situations where they might interfere with the use of the app. |
| WC.10.R004 | dont | Don't display feedback unless it is absolutely necessary. |
| WC.10.R005 | dont | Try not to dramatically customize the visual feedback behaviors of the built-in Windows gestures, as this can create an inconsistent and confusing user experience. |
| WC.10.R006 | note | Contact visualizations are especially critical for touch interactions that require accuracy and precision. |
| WC.10.R007 | note | Using the default XAML platform controls available ensures that your app works correctly on all devices and in all input situations. |
| WC.10.R008 | do | If your app features custom interactions that require customized feedback, you should ensure the feedback is appropriate, spans input devices, and doesn't distract a user from their task. |
| WC.10.R009 | dont | We don't recommend changing the interaction behavior of the built-in gestures. |
| WC.10.R010 | behavior | Visual feedback is generally dependent on the input device (touch, touchpad, mouse, pen/stylus, keyboard, and so on). |
| WC.10.R011 | do | Use ShowGestureFeedback to set feedback behavior for the platform gestures. |
| WC.10.R012 | do | If customizing feedback UI, ensure you provide feedback that supports, and is suitable for, all input modes. |
| WC.10.R013 | definition | There are two parts to the high visibility focus visuals: the primary border and the secondary border. |
| WC.10.R014 | do | To change the thickness of either border type (primary or secondary) use the FocusVisualPrimaryThickness or FocusVisualSecondaryThickness, respectively: |
| WC.10.R015 | definition | The margin is the space between the control's visual bounds and the start of the focus visuals secondary border. |
| WC.10.R016 | do | You can edit this margin on a per-control basis, by changing the FocusVisualMargin property: |
| WC.10.R017 | behavior | A negative margin will push the border away from the center of the control, and a positive margin will move the border closer to the center of the control. |
| WC.10.R018 | do | To turn off focus visuals on the control entirely, simply disabled UseSystemFocusVisuals: |
| WC.10.R019 | behavior | The thickness, margin, or whether or not the app-developer wishes to have the focus visuals at all, is determined on a per-control basis. |
| WC.10.R020 | do | To brand focus visuals app-wide, override the system brushes: |
| WC.10.R021 | do | To change the colors on a per-control basis, just edit the focus visual properties on the desired control: |

#### Buttons - Windows apps

T1 [Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/apps/design/controls/buttons) · updated: 2026-02-28 · 30 rules, 29 values

| ID | Kind | Rule / claim |
|---|---|---|
| WC.10.R022 | do | Use a Button control to let the user initiate an immediate action, such as submitting a form. |
| WC.10.R023 | dont | Don't use a Button control when the action is to navigate to another page; instead, use a HyperlinkButton control. |
| WC.10.R024 | do | For wizard navigation, use buttons labeled Back and Next. |
| WC.10.R025 | do | For other types of backwards navigation or navigation to an upper level, use a back button. |
| WC.10.R026 | do | Use a RepeatButton control when the user might want to trigger an action repeatedly. |
| WC.10.R027 | do | Use a DropDownButton control when the button has a flyout that contains more options. |
| WC.10.R028 | do | Use a SplitButton control when you want the user to be able to initiate an immediate action or choose from additional options independently. |
| WC.10.R029 | do | Use a ToggleButton control when you want the user to be able to immediately switch between two mutually exclusive states, and a button is the best fit for your UI needs. |
| WC.10.R030 | do | Make sure the purpose and state of a button are clear to the user. |
| WC.10.R031 | do | When there are multiple buttons for the same decision, present the commit buttons in this order: OK/[Do it]/Yes, [Don't do it]/No, Cancel. |
| WC.10.R032 | do | Expose only one or two buttons to the user at a time. |
| WC.10.R033 | do | If you need to expose more actions to the user, consider using checkboxes or radio buttons from which the user can select actions, with a single command button to trigger those actions. |
| WC.10.R034 | do | For an action that needs to be available across multiple pages within your app, instead of duplicating a button on multiple pages, consider using a bottom app bar. |
| WC.10.R035 | do | Use concise, specific, self-explanatory button text that clearly describes the action; usually button text is a single word that is a verb. |
| WC.10.R036 | do | Use the default font, unless your brand guidelines tell you to use something different. |
| WC.10.R037 | do | For shorter text, avoid narrow command buttons by using a minimum button width of 120px. |
| WC.10.R038 | do | For longer text, avoid wide command buttons by limiting text to a maximum length of 26 characters. |
| WC.10.R039 | do | Dialogs with only one button should right-align the button, and the button should perform the safe, nondestructive action. |
| WC.10.R040 | do | If your button appears within a container UI, you should right-align the button within the container. |
| WC.10.R041 | do | In pages that contain a single button, left-align the button so it aligns with the rest of the page content. |
| WC.10.R042 | definition | A RepeatButton raises Click events repeatedly from the time it's pressed until it's released. |
| WC.10.R043 | behavior | Set the Delay property to specify the time that the RepeatButton waits after it is pressed before it starts repeating the click action; set Interval to specify the time between repetitions. |
| WC.10.R044 | definition | A DropDownButton shows a chevron as a visual indicator that it has an attached flyout. |
| WC.10.R045 | behavior | A DropDownButton's flyout opens automatically when the button is clicked; typically, use the Flyout property rather than its Click event. |
| WC.10.R046 | behavior | When invoked with touch, a SplitButton behaves as a drop down button; both halves invoke the flyout. |
| WC.10.R047 | behavior | When a SplitButton flyout option is invoked, handle it to both change the selected option and invoke it; the button Click event doesn't occur when using touch. |
| WC.10.R048 | behavior | When invoked with touch, a ToggleSplitButton behaves as a drop down button; both halves invoke the flyout, so include a flyout option to toggle it on or off. |
| WC.10.R049 | claim | ToggleSplitButton does not have an indeterminate state, an IsThreeState property, or an Indeterminate event; IsChecked is Boolean, and it has only IsCheckedChanged rather than Checked and Unchecked events. |
| WC.10.R050 | behavior | When a Button has keyboard focus, pressing Enter or Spacebar raises Click; tapping it or pressing the left mouse button over it also raises Click. |
| WC.10.R051 | behavior | ClickMode defaults to Release; it can also be set to Hover or Press. With Hover, Click cannot be raised by keyboard or touch. |

#### Understanding Styles and Resources in Windows and Uno Platform ...

T4 [nicksnettravels.builttoroam.com](https://nicksnettravels.builttoroam.com/default-winui-styles/) · updated: August 11, 2024 · 18 rules, 7 values

| ID | Kind | Rule / claim |
|---|---|---|
| WC.10.R052 | claim | The generic.xaml file contains nothing more than a dictionary of resources, that are included by default in a Windows App Sdk application that uses Windows UI. |
| WC.10.R053 | definition | The file begins with a set of three ThemeDictionaries, Default (aka Dark), HighContrast and Light. |
| WC.10.R054 | do | Within your application, you can override the system theme by setting the RequestedTheme on elements within your application. |
| WC.10.R055 | behavior | Theme resources ... can be overridden at any level using a technique called light weight styling. |
| WC.10.R056 | claim | It’s not expected that [constant value resources] will be altered based on the theme and it doesn’t seem possible to override the resources and have the updated value take effect within the built in templates and styles. |
| WC.10.R057 | claim | For any given control type, there may be more than on Style defined but there can only be one default Style. |
| WC.10.R058 | definition | It’s the definition of an implicit (unnamed) style that determines the default style for a Button. |
| WC.10.R059 | behavior | A lot of these properties are set to be a theme resource. This means that the property can either be updated by setting the property directly on the Button instance, or it be changed by redefining the theme resource. |
| WC.10.R060 | definition | The ContentPresenter [is] essentially a host control for the Content defined on the Button instance. |
| WC.10.R061 | behavior | The ContentPresenter has properties such as BorderThickness, BorderBrush and Background, which means that the Button ControlTemplate doesn’t need to define a separate element to draw a border or set the background on the content. |
| WC.10.R062 | definition | There is only one VisualStateGroup, CommonStates, that defines the main interaction states of the Button, Normal, PointerOver, Pressed and Disabled. |
| WC.10.R063 | behavior | These states aren’t defined by default because by default the Button style sets the UseSystemFocusVisuals property to true, meaning that focus visuals are applied by the system. |
| WC.10.R064 | do | If you want to provide custom focus visuals, set the UseSystemFocusVisuals property to false, and define a new visual state group with states, Focused, Unfocused and PointerFocused. |
| WC.10.R065 | definition | The PointerOver state defines both some Storyboard and a property Setter. |
| WC.10.R066 | behavior | The first Storyboard ... changes the Background on the ContentPresenter to the ButtonBackgroundPointerOver theme resource. |
| WC.10.R067 | do | You can clone the existing Style and make any changes to either the property setters, or the ControlTemplate. |
| WC.10.R068 | do | You don’t need to clone the entire Style, instead you can base your new Style on the built-in Style (by setting the BasedOn attribute) and then only override the properties you want to modify. |
| WC.10.R069 | do | If you want to understand the default control styles for Windows UI (and Uno Platform) you can inspect the generic.xaml file. |

#### lucentowl.com › study-guides › dotnetStyling, Theming, and Fluent Design - Lucent Owl

T4 [lucentowl.com](https://lucentowl.com/study-guides/dotnet/winui/winui-styling-and-theming.html) · updated: — · 27 rules, 6 values

| ID | Kind | Rule / claim |
|---|---|---|
| WC.10.R070 | definition | WinUI gives an app three ways to change how a control looks: style, lightweight styling, and retemplating; each costs more than the one before it. |
| WC.10.R071 | do | Go only as far down the customization list as the design needs. |
| WC.10.R072 | claim | Microsoft says the best way to stay current with WinUI’s visual styles is to avoid custom styles and templates. |
| WC.10.R073 | behavior | An app that replaces a built-in style or template stops getting changes to it. |
| WC.10.R074 | behavior | A style with an x:Key applies only to controls that set Style to it; a style with no key applies to matching controls in scope that don’t set a style of their own. |
| WC.10.R075 | do | Base a style on Default<ControlName>Style for any WinUI control that provides one. |
| WC.10.R076 | behavior | A class derived from a built-in control needs an implicit style for the new type based on the parent’s default style, or it may not get the WinUI look. |
| WC.10.R077 | behavior | A setter can target only a dependency property. A value set directly on the control wins over the style’s setter for that property. |
| WC.10.R078 | definition | Changing a control’s colors in every state is what lightweight styling is for. |
| WC.10.R079 | behavior | Defining a named resource closer to the control than the WinUI default makes the template use it instead, while retaining the template, states, and accessibility support. |
| WC.10.R080 | do | Put lightweight-style overrides in theme dictionaries, one per theme. |
| WC.10.R081 | behavior | Where overrides live sets how far they reach, following normal resource lookup: control resources affect one control, page resources affect the page, and App.xaml affects the app. |
| WC.10.R082 | do | A real override covers every state the design touches; otherwise the control can revert to a stock color in an uncustomized state. |
| WC.10.R083 | do | Start from a copy of the control’s default template rather than writing one from scratch. |
| WC.10.R084 | do | Keep every name and every state from the copied template, even ones the design doesn’t seem to need. |
| WC.10.R085 | claim | A retemplated control is frozen at the version it was copied from; retemplate only when lightweight styling can’t produce the design. |
| WC.10.R086 | do | App-defined visuals match Fluent Design by using the same named resources instead of hardcoded values. |
| WC.10.R087 | do | Fluent surface and text resources vary by theme, so reference them with {ThemeResource}. |
| WC.10.R088 | claim | Microsoft recommends acrylic for supporting surfaces that overlap content; for a plain vertical pane beside the content, Microsoft recommends an opaque background. |
| WC.10.R089 | behavior | Acrylic turns into a solid color when transparency effects are off, Battery Saver is on, or hardware is low-end; in a Windows contrast theme, the user’s chosen background color replaces it. |
| WC.10.R090 | behavior | Application.RequestedTheme sets the theme for the whole app only at startup; setting it while the app runs throws NotSupportedException. |
| WC.10.R091 | behavior | FrameworkElement.RequestedTheme can change at any time, and its value is inherited unless a descendant sets its own. |
| WC.10.R092 | behavior | Switching the whole window at run time means setting RequestedTheme on the window’s root element, because a WinUI Window has no RequestedTheme. |
| WC.10.R093 | do | An app that offers a theme choice saves it in settings and applies it to the root element at startup and to each new window. |
| WC.10.R094 | behavior | Every {ThemeResource} reference inside an element resolves again when its theme changes; a {StaticResource} reference to a theme-dependent brush keeps its old color. |
| WC.10.R095 | do | Use ActualTheme to find out which theme an element is actually using, and ActualThemeChanged to react when it changes. |
| WC.10.R096 | behavior | A contrast theme overrides RequestedTheme, so anything colored with app-defined resources needs a HighContrast entry in its theme dictionaries. |

### WC.11 Focus visuals


#### Contrast themes - Windows apps \| Microsoft Learn

T1 [Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/apps/design/accessibility/high-contrast-themes) · updated: 08/29/2026 · 27 rules, 5 values

| ID | Kind | Rule / claim |
|---|---|---|
| WC.11.R001 | definition | Contrast themes use a constrained color palette ... to improve legibility, reduce visual fatigue, and support users who rely on high visual separation between foreground and background content. |
| WC.11.R002 | dont | Do not confuse contrast themes with light and dark themes. |
| WC.11.R003 | do | To evaluate your app behavior under contrast themes, enable and customize them in Settings > Accessibility > Contrast themes. |
| WC.11.R004 | behavior | Windows apps enable HighContrastAdjustment by default. This forces white foreground text with a black highlight background to maintain high contrast in all content. |
| WC.11.R005 | do | If your app already uses correct system-aware brushes, set this to None so your intended contrast-theme styling can flow through. |
| WC.11.R006 | behavior | You can programmatically detect contrast mode by using Microsoft.UI.System.ThemeSettings and checking its HighContrast property. |
| WC.11.R007 | do | Use ResourceDictionary.ThemeDictionaries to define theme-specific resources. |
| WC.11.R008 | behavior | This lets your app provide explicit values for Default (Dark), Light, and HighContrast, while still participating in runtime theme switching. |
| WC.11.R009 | do | In Default, define the Brush resources you need, typically SolidColorBrush entries keyed by semantic usage. |
| WC.11.R010 | do | In HighContrast, map each resource to an appropriate dynamic SystemColor value. |
| WC.11.R011 | behavior | Each SystemColor value updates automatically when the user switches themes. |
| WC.11.R012 | do | Test your app in all four built-in contrast themes while it is running. |
| WC.11.R013 | do | Be consistent. |
| WC.11.R014 | do | Set HighContrastAdjustment to None when your theme resources are correctly defined. |
| WC.11.R015 | dont | Do not hard-code colors in HighContrast. Use SystemColor Color and ColorBrush resources. |
| WC.11.R016 | dont | Do not mix incompatible foreground/background pairs. |
| WC.11.R017 | dont | Do not choose resources for aesthetics alone; users can, and do, modify theme colors. |
| WC.11.R018 | dont | Do not use SystemColorGrayTextColor for secondary body text or hint text. Reserve it for disabled content. |
| WC.11.R019 | dont | Do not use SystemColorHotlightColor for anything other than hyperlinks. |
| WC.11.R020 | do | Use {ThemeResource} markup extension to resolve values from ThemeDictionaries in a ResourceDictionary. |
| WC.11.R021 | do | Pages, panes, popups, and bars should generally use SystemColorWindowColor as the background baseline. |
| WC.11.R022 | do | Add contrast-theme-only borders only where you need to preserve important visual boundaries. |
| WC.11.R023 | do | We recommend using 2px borders for transitory surfaces such as flyouts and dialogs. |
| WC.11.R024 | note | Be careful when setting TextBlock.Foreground directly in a DataTemplate for ListView. |
| WC.11.R025 | behavior | ListViewItem sets Foreground for state transitions, and nested TextBlock elements should inherit that value. |
| WC.11.R026 | dont | Explicit Foreground settings break this inheritance chain. |
| WC.11.R027 | do | In HighContrast, omit the explicit Foreground setter so inheritance restores correct inversion behavior. |

| ID | Name | Value | Context |
|---|---|---|---|
| WC.11.V001 | Contrast ratio | typically at or above 7:1 | Contrast themes use a constrained palette with high foreground/background contrast. |
| WC.11.V002 | Built-in contrast themes | 4 | The page names Aquatic, Desert, Dusk, and Night sky. |
| WC.11.V003 | Border thickness | 2px | Recommended for transitory surfaces such as flyouts and dialogs. |
| WC.11.V004 | ThemeResource lookups | twice; two-stage lookup | The example resolves SystemColorWindowColor and BrandedPageBackgroundBrush so the Grid background updates at runtime. |

#### Keyboard interactions - Windows apps

T1 [Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/apps/develop/input/keyboard-interactions) · updated: 09/27/2026 · 35 rules, 3 values

| ID | Kind | Rule / claim |
|---|---|---|
| WC.11.R028 | do | Ensure actionable items are identified as tab stops (and non-actionable items are not), and navigation order is logical and predictable (see Tab stops) |
| WC.11.R029 | do | Set initial focus on the most logical element (see Initial focus) |
| WC.11.R030 | do | Provide arrow key navigation for "inner navigations" (see Navigation) |
| WC.11.R031 | do | Provide accelerator keys for quick actions (see Accelerators) |
| WC.11.R032 | do | Provide access keys to navigate your application's UI (see Access keys) |
| WC.11.R033 | behavior | For a control to be included in the tab order, the IsEnabled property must be set to true and the IsTabStop property must be set to true. |
| WC.11.R034 | do | To specifically exclude a control from the tab order, set the IsTabStop property to false. |
| WC.11.R035 | do | All interactive controls should have tab stops (unless they are in a group), while non-interactive controls, such as labels, should not. |
| WC.11.R036 | dont | Avoid a custom tab order that makes the focus jump around in your application. |
| WC.11.R037 | do | Try to rank and present the most important commands, controls, and content first in both the tab order and the visual order. |
| WC.11.R038 | do | Set initial focus on the UI element for the first, or primary, action that users are most likely to take when launching your app or navigating to a page. |
| WC.11.R039 | dont | Don't set focus to the delete button when navigating to an e-mail. |
| WC.11.R040 | do | Use these default behaviors to optimize your application's keyboard navigation. |
| WC.11.R041 | do | Providing arrow key navigation into a set of related controls reinforces their relationship within the overall organization of your application's UI. |
| WC.11.R042 | do | By defining a single tab stop for a collection of related, or complementary, controls, you can minimize the number of overall tab stops in your app. |
| WC.11.R043 | behavior | For those controls specifically designed for text input such as TextBox and RichEditBox, all keyboard input is used for entering or navigating text, which takes priority over other keyboard commands. |
| WC.11.R044 | behavior | When not in text entry mode, the Space key invokes the action or command associated with the focused control (just like a tap with touch or a click with a mouse). |
| WC.11.R045 | behavior | The Esc key lets a user cancel transient UI (along with any ongoing actions in that UI). |
| WC.11.R046 | behavior | The Esc key only affects transient UI, it does not close, or back navigate through, app UI. |
| WC.11.R047 | do | In addition to implementing keyboard navigation and activation, it is also good practice to implement keyboard shortcuts such as keyboard accelerators and access keys for important or frequently used functionality. |
| WC.11.R048 | definition | Accelerators are shortcuts that invoke an app command. Your app may or may not provide specific UI that corresponds to the command. |
| WC.11.R049 | definition | Access keys are shortcuts that set focus to specific UI in your application. |
| WC.11.R050 | do | Access keys should be assigned consistently to commonly used commands (especially commit buttons), whenever possible. |
| WC.11.R051 | do | For custom layouts and control groups, use XYFocusKeyboardNavigation="Enabled" to provide similar behavior. |
| WC.11.R052 | note | Accessibility users rely on well-established keyboard navigation rules, which do not typically use arrow keys to navigate a collection of buttons. |
| WC.11.R053 | do | In cases where your layout follows a well-known UI pattern for control groups, assigning a single tab stop to the group can improve navigation efficiency for users. |
| WC.11.R054 | do | We recommend a single tab stop for the control group, inner navigation between the radio buttons using the arrow keys, Home key bound to the first radio button, and End key bound to the last radio button. |
| WC.11.R055 | behavior | Text entry requires the user to press A to activate a text control. |
| WC.11.R056 | do | You should ensure that item order does not conflict with this direction. |
| WC.11.R057 | dont | Cycling should be avoided in non-popup UIs where users might come to feel trapped in an endless loop. |
| WC.11.R058 | do | We recommend that you emulate these same behaviors in your custom controls. |
| WC.11.R059 | do | Test your app with all supported input devices to ensure UI elements can be navigated to in a coherent and intuitive way and that no unexpected elements interfere with the desired tab order. |
| WC.11.R060 | behavior | If your app sets focus programmatically to a text input control, the touch keyboard is not invoked. |
| WC.11.R061 | behavior | However, the keyboard does automatically hide when focus is moved programmatically to a non-text input control. |
| WC.11.R062 | definition | Successful keyboard interactions enable users to accomplish basic app scenarios using only the keyboard; that is, users can reach all interactive elements and activate default functionality. |

| ID | Name | Value | Context |
|---|---|---|---|
| WC.11.V005 | F6 | F6 | Keyboard key for cycling between panes or important sections of an app or UI. |
| WC.11.V006 | Close tab shortcut | Ctrl+F4 or Ctrl+W | Common keyboard shortcuts table. |

#### Keyboard accessibility - Windows apps \| Microsoft Learn

T1 [Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/apps/design/accessibility/keyboard-accessibility) · updated: 10/04/2026 · 28 rules, 15 values

| ID | Kind | Rule / claim |
|---|---|---|
| WC.11.R063 | claim | Keyboard accessibility should be treated as a primary interaction model, not a secondary fallback. |
| WC.11.R064 | do | Controls must be focusable and reachable through focus traversal to interact with them using the keyboard. |
| WC.11.R065 | behavior | By default, the tab order of controls is the order in which they are added to a design surface, declared in XAML, or programmatically added to a container. |
| WC.11.R066 | do | Validate tab behavior explicitly. |
| WC.11.R067 | do | Test both keyboard-only and touch interaction paths to ensure traversal remains efficient and predictable. |
| WC.11.R068 | do | To align traversal with visual flow, you can restructure XAML or explicitly assign TabIndex. |
| WC.11.R069 | behavior | Disabled controls are automatically removed from tab order. |
| WC.11.R070 | do | If an element remains interactive through other mechanisms but should not be reached with Tab, set IsTabStop to false. |
| WC.11.R071 | claim | Most focus-capable controls are included in tab order by default. |
| WC.11.R072 | behavior | Text-display controls such as RichTextBlock can support focus for text selection and clipboard operations but are typically not tab stops. |
| WC.11.R073 | behavior | If TabIndex is not set on an element, the default value is Int32.MaxValue, and order is based on declaration/insertion order. |
| WC.11.R074 | behavior | Elements with TabIndex equal to 0 are added based on declaration/insertion order. |
| WC.11.R075 | behavior | Elements with TabIndex greater than 0 are added in ascending TabIndex order. |
| WC.11.R076 | behavior | Elements with TabIndex less than 0 are added before elements with zero values. |
| WC.11.R077 | do | Implement F6 navigation explicitly in your app; it is not provided automatically. |
| WC.11.R078 | do | Shift+F6 should traverse the same cycle in reverse order. |
| WC.11.R079 | do | Where possible, each F6 target region should have a clear accessible name. |
| WC.11.R080 | do | Composite controls should provide predictable inner navigation among child elements. |
| WC.11.R081 | do | Any UI that can be activated by pointer should also be invokable by keyboard. |
| WC.11.R082 | do | For controls that can be invoked, implement keyboard event handlers for the Spacebar and Enter keys. |
| WC.11.R083 | claim | Composition is simpler and more robust than custom pointer-only behavior in many cases. |
| WC.11.R084 | do | Always make shortcuts discoverable for users of assistive technology. |
| WC.11.R085 | claim | Setting AutomationProperties.AcceleratorKey or AutomationProperties.AccessKey does not implement keyboard behavior. |
| WC.11.R086 | do | Use declarative KeyboardAccelerator definitions when possible, and use KeyDown or KeyUp handlers where you need custom routing logic. |
| WC.11.R087 | do | For custom controls, use arrow keys when child elements are spatially related. |
| WC.11.R088 | do | Any focusable custom control should expose a clear visual focus indicator. |
| WC.11.R089 | do | If you retemplate controls, preserve equivalent focus visibility behavior. |
| WC.11.R090 | claim | Coherent tab order is still critical on devices without a physical Tab key. |

| ID | Name | Value | Context |

| WC.11.V007 | TabIndex | 1, 3, 2, 4 | Grid example: GroomFirstName=1, BrideFirstName=3, GroomLastName=2, BrideLastName=4. |
