### WC.12 Win32 / common controls conventions


#### learn.microsoft.com · en-us · windowsControls (Design basics) - Win32 apps \| Microsoft Learn

T1 [Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/uxguide/controls) · updated: 06/04/2021 · 26 rules, 0 values

| ID | Kind | Rule / claim |
|---|---|---|
| WC.12.R001 | note | This design guide was created for Windows 7 and has not been updated for newer versions of Windows. Much of the guidance still applies in principle, but the presentation and examples do not reflect our current design guidance. |
| WC.12.R002 | definition | Controls are UI elements that your users interact with on your app's main window area. |
| WC.12.R003 | behavior | Balloons inform users of a non-critical problem or special condition in a control. |
| WC.12.R004 | behavior | Check boxes allow users to make a decision between two or more clearly differing choices. |
| WC.12.R005 | behavior | Command buttons allow users to perform an immediate action. |
| WC.12.R006 | behavior | Command links allow users to make a choice among a set of mutually exclusive, related choices. |
| WC.12.R007 | behavior | Drop-down lists and combo boxes allow users to make a choice among a list of mutually exclusive values. |
| WC.12.R008 | behavior | Group boxes allow users to see relationships among a set of related controls. |
| WC.12.R009 | behavior | Links allow users to navigate to another page, window, or Help topic; display a definition; initiate a command; or choose an option. |
| WC.12.R010 | behavior | List boxes allow users to select from a set of values presented in a list that is always visible. |
| WC.12.R011 | behavior | With a single-selection list box, users select one item from a list of mutually exclusive values. |
| WC.12.R012 | behavior | With a multiple-selection list box, users select zero or more items from a list of values. |
| WC.12.R013 | behavior | List views allow users to view and interact with a collection of data objects, using either single selection or multiple selection. |
| WC.12.R014 | behavior | Notifications inform users of events that are unrelated to the current user activity. |
| WC.12.R015 | behavior | Progress bars allow users to follow the progress of a lengthy operation. |
| WC.12.R016 | behavior | Progressive disclosure controls allow users to show or hide additional information including data, options, or commands. |
| WC.12.R017 | behavior | Radio buttons allow users to make a choice among a set of mutually exclusive, related choices. |
| WC.12.R018 | behavior | Search boxes provide users a way to locate specific objects or text quickly. |
| WC.12.R019 | behavior | Sliders allow users to choose from a continuous range of values. |
| WC.12.R020 | behavior | Spin controls allow users to change incrementally the value within its associated numeric text box. |
| WC.12.R021 | behavior | Status bars display information about the state of the current window, background tasks, or other contextual information. |
| WC.12.R022 | behavior | Tabs present users with related information on separate labeled pages. |
| WC.12.R023 | behavior | Text boxes allow users to display, enter, or edit a text or numeric value. |
| WC.12.R024 | behavior | Tooltips label an unlabeled control. |
| WC.12.R025 | behavior | Infotips describe an object to which the user is pointing. |
| WC.12.R026 | behavior | Tree views allow users to view and interact with a hierarchically arranged collection of objects, using either single selection or multiple selection. |

#### How to design UX for desktop applications - Win32 apps

T1 [Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/uxguide/how-to-design-desktop-ux) · updated: 02/09/2021 · 25 rules, 0 values

| ID | Kind | Rule / claim |
|---|---|---|
| WC.12.R027 | definition | A great desktop application is powerful and, at the same time, simple. |
| WC.12.R028 | definition | An application is powerful when it enables its target users to realize their full potential efficiently. |
| WC.12.R029 | do | Understand your target users' goals and craft a feature set that enables them to achieve those goals productively. |
| WC.12.R030 | definition | Simplicity is the reduction or elimination of an attribute of a design that target users are aware of and consider unessential. |
| WC.12.R031 | do | Start your design process with the simplest designs that do the job well. |
| WC.12.R032 | do | To obtain simplicity, always design for the probable, not the possible. |
| WC.12.R033 | do | To obtain simplicity, focus on what is likely; reduce, hide, or remove what is unlikely; and eliminate what is impossible. |
| WC.12.R034 | do | Determine the features your users need. |
| WC.12.R035 | do | Remove elements that aren't likely to be used or have preferable alternatives. |
| WC.12.R036 | do | To achieve simplicity, make the hard decision and choose the best one for your target users instead of providing all of them and making the choice an option. |
| WC.12.R037 | do | To achieve simplicity, make it work automatically and either hide it from the user completely or reduce its exposure significantly. |
| WC.12.R038 | do | Put the essential features that support a task together so that a task can be performed in one place. |
| WC.12.R039 | do | Make features that support core scenarios central and obvious, and hide optional functionality or make it peripheral. |
| WC.12.R040 | do | Take a printout of your design and highlight the elements used to perform the most important tasks. |
| WC.12.R041 | do | If you remove the item, would anything bad happen? If not, remove it! |
| WC.12.R042 | do | Essential controls, instructions, and explanations should all be in context in logical order. |
| WC.12.R043 | do | Place less important tasks, options, and Help information outside the main flow in a separate window or page. |
| WC.12.R044 | do | Make tasks discoverable and visible. |
| WC.12.R045 | do | Present tasks in the user's domain. |
| WC.12.R046 | dont | Users shouldn't be required to access external information to use your application successfully. |
| WC.12.R047 | do | Use concepts and terms familiar to your users. |
| WC.12.R048 | do | If a setting has a value that applies to most users in most circumstances, and that setting is both safe and secure, use it as the default value. |
| WC.12.R049 | do | If there are many ways to perform a task, but only some are correct, constrain the task to those correct ways. |
| WC.12.R050 | note | If your "simplification" results in users becoming frustrated, confused, unconfident, or unable to complete tasks successfully, you have removed too much. |
| WC.12.R051 | note | This design guide was created for Windows 7 and has not been updated for newer versions of Windows. Much of the guidance still applies in principle, but the presentation and examples do not reflect our current design guidance. |

#### learn.microsoft.com › win32 › uxguideInteraction - Win32 apps \| Microsoft Learn

T1 [Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/uxguide/interaction) · updated: 06/04/2021 · 24 rules, 8 values

| ID | Kind | Rule / claim |
|---|---|---|
| WC.12.R052 | do | Use these guidelines to create intuitive and distinctive experiences that are optimized for touch but work consistently across input devices. |
| WC.12.R053 | do | First and foremost, design your app with the expectation that touch will be the primary input method of your users. |
| WC.12.R054 | note | If you use the platform controls, support for touchpad, mouse, and pen/stylus requires no additional programming, because Windows provides this for free. |
| WC.12.R055 | dont | Don't take familiar input device properties and behaviors for granted, as touch in Windows 8 does more than simply emulate that functionality. |
| WC.12.R056 | do | Display feedback whenever and wherever touch input is detected, to help the user understand any custom targeting rules that are defined by your app and its controls. |
| WC.12.R057 | do | Size your controls to ensure a comfortable UI with objects and controls that are easy and safe to target. |
| WC.12.R058 | do | Size, space, and position your controls to help eliminate finger and hand occlusion, where the UI is obscured by the user interaction itself. |
| WC.12.R059 | do | Position menus, pop-ups, and tooltips above the contact area whenever possible. |
| WC.12.R060 | do | Avoid, or minimize, sloppy interactions by using UI constraints. |
| WC.12.R061 | behavior | Snap points can make it easier to stop at desired locations. |
| WC.12.R062 | definition | Snap points specify logical stops in your app content. |
| WC.12.R063 | behavior | Cognitively, snap points act as a paging mechanism for the user and minimize fatigue from excessive sliding, swiping, or rotating. |
| WC.12.R064 | do | With them, you can handle imprecise user input and ensure a specific subset of content or key information is displayed. |
| WC.12.R065 | do | Directional "rails" that emphasize the axis of motion (vertical or horizontal). |
| WC.12.R066 | dont | Interactions should not be distinguished by time. |
| WC.12.R067 | do | The same interaction should have the same outcome regardless of the time taken to perform it. |
| WC.12.R068 | note | Time-based activations introduce mandatory delays for users and detract from both the immersive nature of direct manipulation and the perception of system responsiveness. |
| WC.12.R069 | behavior | Timed interactions typically depend on invisible thresholds like time, distance, or speed to determine what command to perform. |
| WC.12.R070 | note | Timed interactions have no visual feedback until the system performs the action and users must reach arbitrary and invisible thresholds to achieve a result. |
| WC.12.R071 | do | Instant visual feedback during interactions make users feel more engaged, confident, and in control. |
| WC.12.R072 | do | Interactions that directly affect objects and mimic real world interactions are more intuitive, discoverable, and memorable. |
| WC.12.R073 | note | They don't rely on obscure or abstract interactions. |
| WC.12.R074 | note | An exception to this is where you use specific timed interactions to assist in learning and exploration (for example, press and hold). |
| WC.12.R075 | do | Using appropriate descriptions and visual cues has a great effect on the use of advanced interactions. |

| ID | Name | Value | Context |
|---|---|---|---|
| WC.12.V001 | Windows version | Windows 7 | The design guide was created for Windows 7. |
| WC.12.V002 | Windows version | Windows 8 | The page says touch in Windows 8 does more than simply emulate familiar input-device functionality. |
| WC.12.V003 | Time span | more than 25 years | The page says indirect input has had the benefit of more than 25 years of refinement. |
| WC.12.V004 | Touch object-state count | two-state | Touch is described as having a two-state model: touched (on) or not (off). |
| WC.12.V005 | Indirect-input object-state count | three-state | Mouse, pen/stylus, and keyboard are described as exposing a three-state model: up (off), down (on), and hover (focus). |
| WC.12.V006 | Input point | single input point | Mouse, keyboard, and pen/stylus interactions are described as supporting a single input point. |

#### UX checklist for desktop applications - Win32 apps

T1 [Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/uxguide/top-violations) · updated: 2021-03-24 · 40 rules, 12 values

| ID | Kind | Rule / claim |
|---|---|---|
| WC.12.R076 | note | This design guide was created for Windows 7 and has not been updated for newer versions of Windows. Much of the guidance still applies in principle, but the presentation and examples do not reflect our current design guidance. |
| WC.12.R077 | do | Support the minimum Windows effective resolution of 800x600 pixels. |
| WC.12.R078 | do | For critical user interfaces (UIs) that must work in safe mode, support an effective resolution of 640x480 pixels. |
| WC.12.R079 | do | Optimize resizable window layouts for an effective resolution of 1024x768 pixels. |
| WC.12.R080 | do | Be sure to test your windows in 96 dots per inch (dpi) (at 800x600 pixels), 120 dpi (at 1024x768 pixels), and 144 dpi (at 1200x900 pixels) modes. |
| WC.12.R081 | do | For programs with touch and mobile use scenarios, optimize for 120 dpi. |
| WC.12.R082 | do | If a window is an owned window, initially display it "centered" on top of the owner window. Never underneath. |
| WC.12.R083 | do | If a window is contextual, always display it near the object that it was launched from. |
| WC.12.R084 | do | Size controls and panes within a window to match their typical content. |
| WC.12.R085 | do | To make text easier to read, consider limiting line widths to 65 characters. |
| WC.12.R086 | do | When a window is resizable and data is truncated, make sure larger window sizes show more data. |
| WC.12.R087 | do | Set a minimum window size if there is a size below which the content is no longer usable. |
| WC.12.R088 | do | Use ordinary, conversational terms when you can. |
| WC.12.R089 | do | Be polite, supportive, and encouraging. |
| WC.12.R090 | do | Remove redundant text. |
| WC.12.R091 | do | Use title-style capitalization for titles, and sentence-style capitalization for all other UI elements. |
| WC.12.R092 | dont | Don't capitalize the names of generic user interface elements, such as toolbar, menu, scroll bar, button, and icon. |
| WC.12.R093 | definition | Ellipses mean incompleteness. |
| WC.12.R094 | dont | Don't use blue text that isn't a link, because users may assume that it is a link. |
| WC.12.R095 | do | Use the main instruction to explain concisely what users should do in a given window or page. |
| WC.12.R096 | do | Label every control or group of controls. |
| WC.12.R097 | do | For all controls, select the safest (to prevent loss of data or system access), most secure value by default. |
| WC.12.R098 | do | Prefer constrained controls. |
| WC.12.R099 | do | Reconsider disabled controls. |
| WC.12.R100 | do | Prefer specific labels over generic ones. |
| WC.12.R101 | do | Always present command links in a set of two or more. |
| WC.12.R102 | do | Provide an explicit Cancel button. Don't use a command link for this purpose. |
| WC.12.R103 | do | Modal dialog boxes require interaction, so use them for things that users must respond to before continuing with their task. |
| WC.12.R104 | do | Use determinate progress bars for operations that require a bounded amount of time, even if that amount of time cannot be accurately predicted. |
| WC.12.R105 | dont | Don't restart progress. |
| WC.12.R106 | do | Use notifications for events that are unrelated to the current user activity, don't require immediate user action, and users can freely ignore. |
| WC.12.R107 | do | Assign initial input focus to the control that users are most likely to interact with first, which is often the first interactive control. |
| WC.12.R108 | do | Tab order should flow from left to right, top to bottom. |
| WC.12.R109 | do | Whenever possible, assign unique access keys to all interactive controls or their labels. |
| WC.12.R110 | dont | Never require users to click an object to determine if it is clickable. |
| WC.12.R111 | do | Use modeless dialog boxes when users need to switch between a dialog box and the owner window. |
| WC.12.R112 | do | Consider lightweight alternatives first, such as dialog boxes, task panes, or single pages. |
| WC.12.R113 | dont | Don't use Welcome pages—make the first page functional whenever possible. |
| WC.12.R114 | do | Whenever possible, propose a solution so users can fix the problem. |
| WC.12.R115 | dont | Don't use unnecessary confirmations. |

| ID | Name | Value | Context |
|---|---|---|---|
| WC.12.V007 | Windows version | Windows 7 | The article notes that the design guide was created for Windows 7. |
| WC.12.V008 | Minimum effective resolution | 800x600 pixels | Recommended minimum Windows effective resolution. |
| WC.12.V009 | Safe-mode effective resolution | 640x480 pixels | Recommended for critical UIs that must work in safe mode. |
| WC.12.V010 | Taskbar space | 48 vertical relative pixels | Reserve this space for windows displayed with the taskbar. |
| WC.12.V011 | Resizable-window target resolution | 1024x768 pixels | Optimize resizable window layouts for this effective resolution. |
| WC.12.V012 | DPI test mode | 96 dpi at 800x600 pixels | One of the specified window-layout test modes. |
| WC.12.V013 | DPI test mode | 120 dpi at 1024x768 pixels | One of the specified window-layout test modes. |
| WC.12.V014 | DPI test mode | 144 dpi at 1200x900 pixels | One of the specified window-layout test modes. |
| WC.12.V015 | Touch and mobile optimization | 120 dpi | Recommended optimization target for programs with touch and mobile use scenarios. |
| WC.12.V016 | Suggested text line width | 65 characters | Consider limiting line widths to make text easier to read. |
| WC.12.V017 | Command-link set size | two or more | Command links should always be presented in a set of two or more. |
| WC.12.V018 | Future sentence spacing | one space | Use one space between sentences. |

#### Visual Styles Overview - Win32 apps - Microsoft Learn

T1 [Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/controls/visual-styles-overview) · updated: 11/04/2020 · 19 rules, 9 values

| ID | Kind | Rule / claim |
|---|---|---|
| WC.12.R116 | definition | A theme is a user-selectable collection of settings that includes wallpaper, cursors, fonts, sounds, and icons. |
| WC.12.R117 | definition | A visual style is a specification that defines the appearance of the Windows common controls. |
| WC.12.R118 | behavior | A .theme file contains a section that specifies the visual style to apply when the particular theme is active. |
| WC.12.R119 | behavior | Users can change the visual style at any time by selecting a different theme. |
| WC.12.R120 | do | You must use the visual styles API to apply the currently active visual style to your application's custom or owner-drawn controls, if any. |
| WC.12.R121 | claim | The information that defines a visual style is stored in a .msstyles file. |
| WC.12.R122 | claim | Microsoft does not support the authoring of .msstyles files. |
| WC.12.R123 | claim | Visual styles are supported by version 6 or later of the common control library (ComCtl32.dll), the visual styles API implemented in UxTheme.dll, the Themes service, and one or more visual style definition files (.msstyles). |
| WC.12.R124 | claim | The visual styles API depends on a system service called Themes. |
| WC.12.R125 | behavior | In Windows 8 and later, the visual styles API still works if the Themes service is off. |
| WC.12.R126 | behavior | In Windows 8 and later, the common controls and the non-client area of windows will still have visual styles when the Themes service is off. |
| WC.12.R127 | claim | In Windows 8, visual styles are always on. |
| WC.12.R128 | do | To use visual styles, your application must be running on an operating system that contains ComCtl32.dll version 6 or later. |
| WC.12.R129 | do | If you want your application to use ComCtl32.dll version 6, you must add an application manifest or compiler directive to specify that version 6 should be used if it is available. |
| WC.12.R130 | claim | For common controls, no further action is necessary to ensure that the controls are displayed in the user's preferred visual style. |
| WC.12.R131 | do | If your application contains custom or owner-drawn controls, you need to use the visual styles API to retrieve information about the currently active visual style, and to draw the controls in that style. |
| WC.12.R132 | claim | For Windows versions prior to Windows 8, applications typically need to provide two separate code paths for drawing custom and owner-drawn controls. |
| WC.12.R133 | claim | In Windows 8, separate theming code paths are not needed. |
| WC.12.R134 | claim | Applications that are manifested for Windows 8 get high contrast theming "for free." |

| ID | Name | Value | Context |
|---|---|---|---|
| WC.12.V019 | Theme introduction | Windows 95 | Themes were introduced in Microsoft Plus! for Windows 95. |
| WC.12.V020 | Windows Vista | Windows Vista | Theme files are displayed in the Personalization control panel in Windows Vista and later. |
| WC.12.V021 | Windows 7 | Windows 7 | Example desktop using the Windows Aero theme; the Themes service rendering description also applies through Windows 7. |
| WC.12.V022 | Windows 8 | Windows 8 | Visual styles are described as always on in Windows 8; the API behavior with the Themes service off is described for Windows 8 and later. |
| WC.12.V023 | Windows 11 | Windows 11 | Appears in the user’s topic description, not in the page content. |
| WC.12.V024 | Common control library version | Version 6 or later | ComCtl32.dll version required to use visual styles. |
| WC.12.V025 | ComCtl32.dll version | Version 6 | Version to specify in an application manifest or compiler directive if available. |

#### Support Dark and Light themes in Win32 apps - Microsoft Learn

T1 [Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/apps/desktop/modernize/ui/apply-windows-themes) · updated: 06/16/2026 · 12 rules, 8 values

| ID | Kind | Rule / claim |
|---|---|---|
| WC.12.R135 | definition | Light mode generally uses black or dark text on white or light backgrounds; Dark mode generally uses white or light text on black or dark backgrounds. |
| WC.12.R136 | do | Foreground elements, highlights, and text should be closer to the foreground color than the background color. |
| WC.12.R137 | do | Large, solid background areas and text backgrounds should generally be closer to the background color than the foreground color. |
| WC.12.R138 | claim | To fully support Dark mode, the entirety of an app's surface must follow the dark theme. |
| WC.12.R139 | do | A Win32 app should read the color setting at startup and detect when the setting changes during an app session. |
| WC.12.R140 | behavior | The article uses the system foreground color to determine whether Dark mode is enabled: a light foreground indicates Dark mode. |
| WC.12.R141 | claim | The IsColorLight calculation is a quick brightness estimate, not a model for real analysis of color brightness. |
| WC.12.R142 | behavior | Windows gives Win32 apps a light title bar by default for backwards compatibility because it does not know whether an app supports Dark mode. |
| WC.12.R143 | do | For an app that uses the standard Windows title bar, call DwmSetWindowAttribute with DWMWA_USE_IMMERSIVE_DARK_MODE to request a dark title bar. |
| WC.12.R144 | do | Set the BOOL passed to DwmSetWindowAttribute to TRUE to honor Dark mode; set it to FALSE for Light mode. |
| WC.12.R145 | claim | The article does not cover how to repaint and render the app UI using a Dark mode color set. |
| WC.12.R146 | claim | Windows App SDK supports Dark mode natively and changes certain UI elements without additional code. |

#### Welcome to the Dark Side of Windows

| WC.12.R148 | claim | We’ve added support for dark mode aware Windows apps. |
| WC.12.R149 | claim | Microsoft has always provided enough capabilities (for the most part) to get Win32 controls themed for dark mode. |
| WC.12.R150 | claim | Some Win32 controls they even themed correctly from the beginning when Microsoft first introduced dark mode on Windows. |
| WC.12.R151 | claim | There are a few controls that are not supported in dark mode, such as TabPanels, autocompleting ComboBoxes, and DateTimePicker. |
| WC.12.R152 | behavior | To opt into dark mode, just enable it in the shared build settings (by default this is enabled). |

| WC.12.R154 | do | When you are ready for dark mode, make any necessary image adjustments and make sure to use the appropriately themed colors (for example Color.TextColor) in places that need it. |
| WC.12.R155 | do | For dark mode customization use the Color.IsDarkMode to check if the OS is in dark mode. |
| WC.12.R156 | do | To theme colors differently between light and dark mode, add a ColorGroup and assign them to the controls that need them. |
| WC.12.R157 | behavior | You can disable dark mode theming (even when you have the dark mode support enabled) in your app by setting up the XOJO_WIN32_DARKMODE_DISABLED environment variable (either set to “true” or “1”) in your App.Opening event. |

#### Guidelines for progress controls - Windows apps \| Microsoft Learn

T1 [Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/apps/develop/ui/controls/progress-controls) · updated: 07/08/2026 · 29 rules, 3 values

| ID | Kind | Rule / claim |
|---|---|---|
| WC.13.R001 | definition | A progress control provides feedback to the user that a long-running operation is underway. |
| WC.13.R002 | do | The determinate state for both ProgressBar and ProgressRing shows the percentage completed of a task. This should be used during an operation whose duration is known, but its progress should not block the user's interaction with the app. |
| WC.13.R003 | definition | The indeterminate state for ProgressBar shows that an operation is underway, does not block user interaction with the app, and its completion time is unknown. |
| WC.13.R004 | definition | The indeterminate state for ProgressRing shows that an operation is underway, blocks user interaction with the app, and its completion time is unknown. |
| WC.13.R005 | behavior | Additionally, a progress control is read only and not interactive. Meaning that the user cannot invoke or use these controls directly. |
| WC.13.R006 | do | Use a determinate ProgressBar then, and update the percentage or value accordingly. |
| WC.13.R007 | behavior | When a ProgressBar is in use, interaction is non-modal, typically meaning that the user is not blocked by that operation's completion, and can continue to use the app in other ways until that aspect has completed. |
| WC.13.R008 | do | If your operation falls around these keywords, or if you're showing text that alongside the progress operation that matches these keywords; consider using a ProgressBar: |
| WC.13.R009 | do | If an operation requires all (or a large portion) of interaction with the app to wait until it has been completed, then the indeterminate ProgressRing is the better choice. |
| WC.13.R010 | do | Use a determinate ProgressRing if you want the visual to be a ring instead of a bar, and update the percentage or value accordingly. |
| WC.13.R011 | do | If so, use an indeterminate ProgressRing, as they're meant to indicate an unknown wait time for the user. |
| WC.13.R012 | do | If your operation falls around these keywords, or if you're showing text alongside the progress operation that matches these keywords; consider using a ProgressRing: |
| WC.13.R013 | note | For example, if the app is downloading something in the background and the user didn't initiate the download, the user doesn't necessarily need to know about it. |
| WC.13.R014 | do | Use text when your app is performing tasks that don't have to be visible all the time, but you still need to show the status. |
| WC.13.R015 | do | Sometimes it's best to show a notice only when the operation is completed, or give a visual that the operation has been completed immediately, and run the finishing touches in the background. |
| WC.13.R016 | do | When the duration of the operation is known, when installing, downloading, setting up, etc; a determinate ProgressBar is best. |
| WC.13.R017 | do | When it is not known how long the operation will take, use an indeterminate ProgressBar. |
| WC.13.R018 | do | Indeterminate ProgressBars are also good when filling a virtualized list, and creating a smooth visual transition between an indeterminate to determinate ProgressBar. |
| WC.13.R019 | dont | If so, do not put a progress indicator on each list item as they appear. Instead, use a ProgressBar and place it at the top of the collection of items being loaded in, to show that the items are being fetched. |
| WC.13.R020 | do | The indeterminate ProgressRing is used when any further user interaction with the app is halted, or the app is waiting for the user's input to continue. |
| WC.13.R021 | do | When the duration of the operation is known and the ring visual is desired, when installing, downloading, setting up, etc; a determinate ProgressRing is best. |
| WC.13.R022 | do | In order to resize a ProgressRing, you must set its height and width. |
| WC.13.R023 | behavior | If only height or width are set, the control will assume minimum sizing (20x20epx) – conversely if the height and width are set to two different sizes, the smaller of the sizes will be assumed. |
| WC.13.R024 | do | To ensure your ProgressRing is correct for your needs, set both the height and the width to the same value: |
| WC.13.R025 | do | To make your ProgressRing visible, and animate, you must set the IsActive property to true: |
| WC.13.R026 | behavior | By default, the main coloring of the progress controls is set to the accent color of the system. |
| WC.13.R027 | do | To override this brush simply change the foreground property on either control. |
| WC.13.R028 | do | The foreground property for the ProgressBar will change the fill color of the bar – to alter the unfilled portion of the bar, simply override the background property. |
| WC.13.R029 | do | Sometimes it's best to just show a brief wait cursor, when the app or operation needs time to think, and you need to indicate to the user that the app or area where the wait cursor is visible should not be interacted with until the wait cursor has disappeared. |

| ID | Name | Value | Context |
|---|---|---|---|
| WC.13.V001 | Minimum ProgressRing size | 20x20epx | The ProgressRing can be sized as large as you want, but can only be as small as 20x20epx. |
| WC.13.V002 | Height | 100 | ProgressRing XAML examples set Height="100". |
| WC.13.V003 | Width | 100 | ProgressRing XAML examples set Width="100"; the ProgressBar coloring example sets Width="100". |

APIs: `ProgressBar`, `ProgressRing`, `ProgressBar class`, `ProgressRing class`, `IsIndeterminate`, `IsActive`, `Height`, `Width`, `Foreground`, `Background`, `ProtectedCursor`, `InputSystemCursor`, `InputSystemCursor.Create`, `InputSystemCursorShape.Wait`, `WaitProgressRing`

#### InfoBar - Windows apps

T1 [Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/apps/develop/ui/controls/infobar) · updated: 02/25/2026 · 39 rules, 8 values

| ID | Kind | Rule / claim |
|---|---|---|
| WC.13.R030 | do | Use an InfoBar control when a user should be informed of, acknowledge, or take action on a changed application state. |
| WC.13.R031 | behavior | An InfoBar will take up space in your layout and behave like any other child elements. |
| WC.13.R032 | behavior | It will not cover up other content or float on top of it. |
| WC.13.R033 | dont | Do not use an InfoBar control to confirm or respond directly to a user action that doesn't change the state of the app, for time-sensitive alerts, or for non-essential messages. |
| WC.13.R034 | do | Use an InfoBar that is closed by the user or when the status is resolved for scenarios that directly impact app perception or experience. |
| WC.13.R035 | do | Use an InfoBar that is closed by the user for scenarios that indirectly impact app perception or experience. |
| WC.13.R036 | do | For scenarios where a persistent notification is not needed, e.g. displaying information in context of a specific UI element, a Flyout is a better option. |
| WC.13.R037 | do | For scenarios where the application is confirming a user action, showing information the user must read, use a ContentDialog. |
| WC.13.R038 | do | If a status change to the app is so severe that it needs to block all further ability for the user to interact with the app, use a ContentDialog. |
| WC.13.R039 | do | For scenarios where a notification is a transient teaching moment, a TeachingTip is a better option. |
| WC.13.R040 | dont | The InfoBar should not appear and disappear from view rapidly to prevent flashing on the screen. |
| WC.13.R041 | dont | Avoid flashing visuals for people with photosensitivities and to improve the usability of your application. |
| WC.13.R042 | do | For InfoBars that automatically enter and exit the view via an app status condition, we recommend you include logic in your application to prevent content from appearing or disappearing rapidly or multiple times in a row. |
| WC.13.R043 | do | In general, this control should be used for long-lived status messages. |
| WC.13.R044 | do | If you would like to inform users that use screen readers of the InfoBar's updated content we recommend you close and re-open the control to trigger the event. |
| WC.13.R045 | note | For InfoBars that are inline with other UI content, keep in mind how the rest of the page will responsively react to the addition of the element. |
| WC.13.R046 | note | If the InfoBar appears or disappears rapidly, especially in succession, the user may be confused with the changing visual state. |
| WC.13.R047 | note | When customizing the color and icon outside of the preset Severity levels, keep in mind user expectations for the connotations from the set of standard icons and colors. |
| WC.13.R048 | do | We recommend to use these colors when possible and to include custom logic in your application to adapt to the various color states and accessibility features. |
| WC.13.R049 | dont | Avoid setting the Severity property for a notification that does not match the information communicated in the Title, Message, or custom content. |
| WC.13.R050 | definition | Error: An error or problem that has occurred. |
| WC.13.R051 | definition | Warning: A condition that might cause a problem in the future. |
| WC.13.R052 | definition | Success: A long-running and/or background task has completed. |
| WC.13.R053 | definition | Default: General information that requires the user's attention. |
| WC.13.R054 | dont | Icons and color should not be the only UI components signifying meaning for your notification. |
| WC.13.R055 | do | Text in the notification's Title and/or Message should be included to display information. |
| WC.13.R056 | dont | We recommend you avoid positioning based on message length or other UI elements set to a specific language. |
| WC.13.R057 | behavior | The notification will follow standard mirroring behavior when localized to/from languages that are right to left (RTL) or left to right (LTR). |
| WC.13.R058 | behavior | By default, the InfoBar will not be visible. |
| WC.13.R059 | behavior | An InfoBar can be closed by the user via the close button or programmatically. |
| WC.13.R060 | do | If the notification is required to be in view until the status is resolved and you would like to remove the ability for the user to close the info bar, you can set the IsClosable property to false. |
| WC.13.R061 | behavior | A custom background color can be set via the standard Background property and will override the color set by Severity. |
| WC.13.R062 | note | Please keep in mind content readability and accessibility when setting your own color. |
| WC.13.R063 | behavior | A custom icon can be set via the IconSource property. |
| WC.13.R064 | behavior | By default, an icon will be visible (assuming the control isn't collapsed). |
| WC.13.R065 | behavior | Custom styling will be applied to action buttons of type Button and HyperlinkButton for consistency and accessibility. |
| WC.13.R066 | behavior | XAML content can be added to an InfoBar using the Content property. It will appear in its own line below the rest of the control content. |
| WC.13.R067 | behavior | The Closing event can be used to cancel and/or defer the close of an InfoBar. |
| WC.13.R068 | behavior | When the closing of an InfoBar is canceled, IsOpen will go back to true. |

| ID | Name | Value | Context |
|---|---|---|---|
| WC.13.V004 | Subscription expiration example | 3 days | Example InfoBar message: “Your subscription is expiring in 3 days.” |
| WC.13.V005 | Custom icon recommended size | 20px | The recommended icon size for custom icons. |
| WC.13.V006 | Terms and Conditions sample link text | Sep 2020 | Sample HyperlinkButton content. |
| WC.13.V007 | ProgressBar MaxWidth | 200 | XAML example sets MaxWidth="200". |
| WC.13.V008 | ProgressBar Margin | 0,0,0,6 | XAML example sets Margin="0,0,0,6". |

#### Dialog controls - Windows apps - Microsoft Learnlearn.microsoft.com › design › controls › dialogs-and-flyouts › dialogs

T1 [Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/apps/design/controls/dialogs-and-flyouts/dialogs) · updated: 07/15/2026 (shown for the Learning path card; no article-specific update date is shown) · 26 rules, 5 values

| ID | Kind | Rule / claim |
|---|---|---|
| WC.13.R069 | do | Use dialogs to notify users of important information or to request confirmation or additional info before an action can be completed. |
| WC.13.R070 | do | Clearly identify the issue or the user's objective in the first line of the dialog's text. |
| WC.13.R071 | definition | The dialog title is the main instruction and is optional. |
| WC.13.R072 | do | If you're using the dialog to deliver a simple message, error or question, you can optionally omit the title. |
| WC.13.R073 | definition | The dialog content contains the descriptive text and is required. |
| WC.13.R074 | do | At least one dialog button must appear. |
| WC.13.R075 | do | Ensure that your dialog has at least one button corresponding to a safe, nondestructive action like "Got it!", "Close", or "Cancel". |
| WC.13.R076 | do | Use specific responses to the main instruction or content as button text. |
| WC.13.R077 | do | Ensure that the text of the action buttons is concise. |
| WC.13.R078 | do | The "do it" action button(s) should appear as the leftmost buttons. The safe, nondestructive action should appear as the rightmost button. |
| WC.13.R079 | dont | Don't use dialogs for errors that are contextual to a specific place on the page, such as validation errors (in password fields, for example), use the app's canvas itself to show inline errors. |
| WC.13.R080 | do | Use the ContentDialog class to build your dialog experience. Don't use the deprecated MessageDialog API. |
| WC.13.R081 | do | In WinUI 3, you must set the XamlRoot property on the ContentDialog before calling ShowAsync. |
| WC.13.R082 | behavior | There can only be one ContentDialog open per window at a time. Attempting to open two content dialogs will throw an exception. |
| WC.13.R083 | behavior | When the user clicks a dialog button, the ShowAsync method returns a ContentDialogResult to let you know which button the user clicks. |
| WC.13.R084 | do | All dialogs should contain at least one safe action button to close the dialog. |
| WC.13.R085 | definition | The CloseButton represents the safe, nondestructive action that enables the user to exit the dialog. Appears as the rightmost button. |
| WC.13.R086 | definition | PrimaryButton represents the first "do it" action. Appears as the leftmost button. |
| WC.13.R087 | definition | SecondaryButton represents the second "do it" action. Appears as the middle button. |
| WC.13.R088 | claim | Using the built-in buttons will position the buttons appropriately, ensure that they correctly respond to keyboard events, ensure that the command area remains visible even when the on-screen keyboard is up, and will make the dialog look consistent with other dialogs. |
| WC.13.R089 | behavior | Pressing on the CloseButton returns ContentDialogResult.None. |
| WC.13.R090 | behavior | When the user clicks on the PrimaryButton, the ShowAsync method returns ContentDialogResult.Primary. |
| WC.13.R091 | behavior | When the user clicks on the SecondaryButton, the ShowAsync method returns ContentDialogResult.Secondary. |
| WC.13.R092 | behavior | Specifying the default button causes the following to happen: the button receives the Accent Button visual treatment; it responds to the ENTER key automatically, subject to the stated focus exception; and it receives focus automatically when the dialog opens unless the dialog's content contains focusable UI. |
| WC.13.R093 | claim | By default, no default button is set. |
| WC.13.R094 | do | In general, the affirmation button should be on the left (the primary button) and the cancel button (the secondary button) should be on the right. |

| ID | Name | Value | Context |
|---|---|---|---|
| WC.13.V010 | one ContentDialog | 1 | Only one ContentDialog can be open per window at a time. |
| WC.13.V011 | dialog buttons | At least 1 | At least one dialog button must appear. |
| WC.13.V012 | optional action buttons | 1 or 2 | In addition to the safe, nondestructive action, the dialog may present one or two action buttons. |
| WC.13.V013 | dialog button types | 3 | ContentDialog has three different types of buttons: CloseButton, PrimaryButton, and SecondaryButton. |
| WC.13.V014 | subscription price | $9.99/month | Example dialog offers a subscription for $9.99/month. |
